## AI Research

### [Contra Wentworth on Physical Attractiveness for Men](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ytzrakjgcvCfLCCZp/contra-wentworth-on-physical-attractiveness-for-men)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>This is a response to John Wentworth’s recent article, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/w3y9G4ybNb3rmTgev/why-physical-attractiveness-matters-for-men-s-dating"><span>Why Physical Attractiveness Matters for Men’s Dating Prospects</span></a><span>. I have no quibble with the thesis stated in the title, but a lot of the body of the article struck me as off-base. When John sent me the article, I told him the article seemed “fundamentally confused.” He asked for details, and this article is my answer.</span></p><p><span>Here I list and argue with four premises I detected in John’s article. Some of them he said in so many words; some are my (possibly-incorrect) inferences.</span></p><p><span>I have my own weird perspective on a lot of this stuff that isn’t necessarily to be trusted. To avoid just arguing purely from that weird perspective, I ran several </span><a href="http://glosso.ink"><span>glosso</span></a><span> surveys to gather data. Glosso data itself is pretty sus! I could only survey people’s self-reports, which could easily be inaccurate. The surveys have pretty low N. The population on glosso is itself weird; it’s Aella’s social network, originally seeded with Aella’s slutty, high-body-count, looks-conscious friends. (I say this with love, as someone pretty firmly rooted in that social network, sharing those characteristics!) So we shouldn’t put too much weight on any of this data. I used this data source because it was readily available and better than just going with N=1.</span></p><h1><span>Premise #1: Eye contact is typically on the path from “we’re strangers” to “we’re flirting”</span></h1><h2><span>One woman’s ungeneralizable field report</span></h2><p><span>In John’s article, he seems to take it as a given that eye contact is on the path from “we’re strangers” to “we’re flirting.” This premise was the one that stood out the most to me as misguided.</span></p><p><span>I flirt a lot. I get to know people pretty easily. I’ve navigated the strangers-to-flirting transition hundreds of times. But I can’t think of a single time in my entire life that it started with wordless eye contact.</span></p><p><span>Maybe it’s just me? Maybe I’m the weird one? But this is where I wanted to start my investigation.</span></p><p><span>First, why doesn’t making a connection run through eye contact for </span><i><span>me</span></i><span>?</span></p><p><span>Well, because eye contact is way too fucking intimate of a place to start, that’s why. If it’s literally the first thing that happens, it’s opening the door to who-even-knows-what, and the odds of me liking the results are very low. Most humans, drawn from the population at large, are not sexually attractive to me, and I can’t tell which ones are actually interesting just by looking at them.</span></p><p><span>Eye contact is an </span><i><span>invitation</span></i><span>. It says, “Come over and talk to me, I’m already interested in you, you’re not going to get shot down.”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3xjo6m2misa"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3xjo6m2misa">[1]</a></sup></span><span> And that’s just not true. I’m </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> already interested in him. I probably </span><i><span>am </span></i><span>going to shoot him down. I don’t want to engage in false advertising like that, and I don’t want to suffer through a horrible, awkward conversation that I brought upon myself.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8yio773fz4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8yio773fz4">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>So no, I’m definitely never going to start there. I’m going to gather information some other way first.</span></p><p><span>Let’s say the info-gathering goes well, and I do want to initiate contact. What then?</span></p><p><span>Well, I personally am not going to be coy about it. Making eye contact seems kind of hard to do, easy to miss, and easy to misinterpret. I’m going to </span><i><span>go talk to him</span></i><span>. I do think I’m weird in this way, I think many women have much more skill at non-verbal flirtation than I do. Plausibly I could work on it. But maybe looking at the inner workings of one direct and autistic girl will tell you something about other women… especially if, perhaps, dating direct and autistic girls does tend to work out well for you in the end. Hypothetically.</span></p><p><br /></p><h2><span>Okay, but what about other women, though</span></h2><p><span>So… how typically </span><i><span>is</span></i><span> eye contact on the strangers-to-flirting path? I turned to glosso to find out.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779836975/lexical_client_uploads/jslpb2pnh3ceejpul2ii.png" /><p><br /></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779836975/lexical_client_uploads/iam02hnminxzz7lucggj.png" /><p><br /></p><p><span>This paints a picture that goes something like this:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>she notices you and is attracted to you (a given of the questions)</span></li><li value="2"><span>she probably takes a non-obvious action to gather more info about you (54% non-obvious vs. 28% obvious)</span></li><li value="3"><span>once she decides she’s willing to make contact,</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>~50% chance she takes an obvious action (makes eye contact, goes up and talks to you)</span></li><li value="2"><span>~50% chance she takes a more subtle or invisible action (joins the same conversation group, becomes receptive to your advances)</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>I note again the the sample size is very small here, but the trends have seemed reasonably stable as data has accrued. If anything changes wildly as more data accumulates I will update this post.</span></p><h2><span>A richer model of initiating contact</span></h2><p><span>It seems to me that, even for women who give a lot of weight to physical attraction, you’ve got to give them more to go on before any of them are going to be making eyes at you – and some never will. You need to facilitate their info-gathering, so they’re predisposed to receive your overtures well. And even with that, you probably need to make the first move, unless you are trying to date me specifically.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefo35n1xvdby"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fno35n1xvdby">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>And if you’re John-like, you want to do all of that efficiently: you want a decent ROI on your efforts.</span></p><p><span>You’re going to need a better working model of where to direct your efforts.</span></p><p><span>We’ll come back to that later.</span></p><h1><span>Premise #2: Being physically attractive works on enough of the right women to make it a viable mainline strategy</span></h1><p><span>John’s argument here is that physical attraction matters. It matters to a large fraction of women, and it matters to the women that a John-like guy wants to meet.</span></p><h2><span>How big a factor is physical attraction?</span></h2><p><span>This one always sounds fake to me, because physical attraction is pretty low on my own personal list; I tend to run much more on the sorts of characteristics you notice by reading someone’s blog. But people vary, and I do see some of my female friends filtering on physical attractiveness. How much does it matter, to how many people?</span></p><p><span>First I just straight-up asked.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779836975/lexical_client_uploads/u4cqbdeqcyf3ezzulme9.png" /><p><span>The mean for women was 38%; the mean for men was 56%.</span></p><p><span>My own personal answer to this is somewhere in the bottom two buckets (0-15%); it depends a lot on context. I expected other women to weight it higher than I did, and I expected men to weight it higher than women did, and I was right, but I was surprised by the magnitude on both counts. What are you people even doing. The quality of the blog really matters and I will die on this hill.</span></p><p><span>Anyway.</span></p><h2><span>How efficient is physical attraction at getting you laid?</span></h2><p><span>Next, I wanted to know how much that physical attraction translates into action. John’s post is saturated with the desire for efficiency. I don’t think he’s saying that every lead needs to convert to sex, but I do have the impression he’s pretty sex-driven, and that connections that are going to be impossible or very difficult to convert to sex over time are less interesting.</span></p><p><span>I had a guess. Maybe if a woman’s decision-making is fueled significantly by physical attraction, then she really doesn’t need a lot of time to make a decision; she already knows most of what she needs to know in the first five seconds. To be generous, I decided to give her five full hours to take a really good look.</span></p><p><span>My model of John is interested in women who make up their minds about connections quickly, so one important question is: are the quick ones driven significantly by looks?</span></p><p><span>My guess was that there would be two clusters: women who make sexual connections quickly and easily, with an emphasis on looks, and women whose discriminate mostly on factors that take longer to assess.</span></p><p><span>(My personal answer: I’m quick, and it’s less hotness-driven. I’m a fast and decisive blog-reader.)</span></p><p><span>So I asked:</span></p><p><b><span>Women: Do you typically have sex with a new partner in less than 5 hours of spending time with them? And is your decision to have sex more than 30% based on their looks? </span></b><br /></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>More hotness-driven (&gt;30%)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Less hotness-driven (&lt;30%)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Total by speed</span></b></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Faster (&lt;5 hrs)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>13%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>8%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>21%</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Slower (&gt;5 hrs)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>26%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>34%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>60%</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Total by hotness-driven</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>39%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>42%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>I don't know</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>18%</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><br /></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><br /></p><p><span>(Note: there’s a disparity between these two batches of results; both questions agree that ~40% of women believe that less than 30% of their attraction was about looks. But the previous question had 59% saying that 30% or more of their attraction was about looks, vs. 42% saying that here, and 18% saying “I don’t know.” Perhaps the women who are more into physical attraction also had more trouble understanding the 2x2-formatted question. Or perhaps that’s just me being unnecessarily snarky.)</span></p><h2><span>Physical attraction does seem like a viable pathway, but to a narrower target</span></h2><p><span>My takeaways from these two questions are that physical attraction matters a lot to some women, and those women are disproportionately likely to act quickly on that attraction.</span></p><p><span>This premise is holding up okay!</span></p><p><span>It’s important to note, though, that you’ve narrowed the pool of women pretty significantly if you focus on this pathway specifically – but maybe that’s the right trade to make.</span></p><h1><span>Premise #3: The effort-to-results curve on making yourself physically attractive is favorable</span></h1><p><span>John’s premise here is: “You can pay an upfront cost to get in good shape, dress well, etc. You do it basically once.” And then you get results.</span></p><p><span>But do you, though? What’s the effort-to-results curve on this?</span></p><p><span>Let’s take a bunch of components of “becoming physically attractive” and order them by how much investment they will require. Cheap and easy ones first, difficult ones at the end. Here’s the list I made after thinking about it for less than two minutes.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>hygiene (be clean, smell nice)</span></li><li value="2"><span>grooming (good hairstyle, facial hair that suits you)</span></li><li value="3"><span>clothing (fits you well, looks good)</span></li><li value="4"><span>weight (try GLP-1s if you are fat, but they don’t work for everyone)</span></li><li value="5"><span>muscles (effortful, takes upkeep. don’t overdo it!)</span></li><li value="6"><span>teeth (e.g. orthodontia or dental veneers. expensive)</span></li><li value="7"><span>face (e.g. plastic surgery. expensive, invasive, not always possible to do much)</span></li><li value="8"><span>height (no currently known solution)</span></li></ul><p><span>The question is, how far down this list can you afford to go, and will that be enough? And the answer to that depends in part on what raw materials you’re starting with. Is “hot” even achievable for you? I have no idea, it varies a lot! Maybe you got lucky and you don’t need the help with the last three or four things on this list. That’s great!</span></p><p><span>Probably everyone who is in the dating market should do the first few things on the list, but if you are short and have an unfortunate face, then that’s bad luck. It is not the slightest bit your fault, but it does make the entire “just be hot” strategy much less viable for you. I’m sorry.</span></p><p><span>So does this premise hold up? Probably for some people! To read John’s accounts, it does not actually hold up for him. He’s in great shape and he already dresses well, but he reports that no strangers are making eye contact with him. (Sorry, John, I don’t mean to pick on you, but your data does seem to directly contradict your thesis, here.) </span></p><h1><span>Premise #4: Being physically attractive saves you other kinds of time and effort</span></h1><p><span>This argument, as I understood it, rests on a flirtation model that goes:</span></p><p><span>	</span><span>physically attractive → woman likes you → eye contact → flirtation → victory</span></p><p><span>John posed several alternative ways to make initial contact that he said “sucked ass,” such as spending social time around women so they could get to know him, learning to hit on women through banter and giving compliments, or waiting until late at night when the women were drunk and sleepy and would be amenable to hooking up.</span></p><p><span>The promise of being physically attractive is that you could skip all that, because women would decide they liked you based on looks alone.</span></p><p><span>This line of reasoning falls down in two places already covered by graphs above.</span></p><p><span>First, when a woman finds you physically attractive, she doesn’t </span><i><span>like</span></i><span> you yet, she just finds you hot. And as per the data above, her next move is not usually to make eye contact, it’s to gather more information somehow. In many cases, she’s observing you, trying to decide if she’s going to signal overtly or not. Whatever she’s looking for… is she going to see it? You’re going to have to attend to that, and some of the best ways to attend to it are by being pleasantly social in ways that, to John, suck ass.</span></p><p><span>Second, you still have to get from “flirtation” to “victory,” whether victory is setting up a second encounter, taking her home that night, or whatever. And yes, I get that’s </span><i><span>much </span></i><span>easier than getting the flirtation started in the first place. I concede the point that the initial contact is the hardest. But still… there is still some form of attraction she’s still looking for at that point; 75% of women surveyed said they derived </span><i><span>half or more</span></i><span> of their decision to get closer to factors other than physical attraction. So you’re going to have to round out your game somehow.</span></p><p><span>The premise that you can save a tremendous amount of time does not seem to hold up.</span></p><h1><span>My prescription: Be well rounded. No, not like that.</span></h1><p><span>What you want is to be attractive in many ways, while not being obviously unattractive in any ways, and do all that efficiently, so you can keep your prospect-funnel full cheaply, if you know what I mean.</span></p><p><span>I think it is most useful to think across many dimensions at once, and buy cheap and easy results everywhere simultaneously. Once that’s done, maybe you’re already seeing the results you want, or maybe you specialize further.</span></p><p><span>So yes, do the first half of the physical attractiveness list, by all means. But also do the first half of the “confidence and poise” list and the the “good banter” list and the “putting people at ease” list and the “projecting personality” list and the “good listener” list and the “good social reputation” list and the “checks out online” list and probably a bunch more. The construction of those lists is currently left as an exercise for the reader.</span></p><p><span>And finally, consider getting some wingwomen on your side. Have them spy on you in social situations and provide field notes on how you come across, which lists to prioritize. What’s already going well for you, and where are you losing prospects? Try to figure that out.</span></p><p><span>Spending more time at the gym might be a way of avoiding working on the actual bottlenecks in your game.</span></p><p><br /></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3xjo6m2misa"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3xjo6m2misa">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span> If you make eye contact at a sex club or a sex party, eye contact is an even bigger invitation than that.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8yio773fz4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8yio773fz4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Other women say this more strongly. They are worried that the guy will be “a creep.” I haven’t done a field study on what they mean by this, but I think it means that the guy will assume too much, get in their space, try to get handsy, stuff like that. Making eye contact with potential creeps doesn’t just feel potentially awkward, it feels </span><i><span>unsafe.</span></i></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fno35n1xvdby"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefo35n1xvdby">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>With me specifically, the outcome of the game has usually been decided before you have even considered your first move.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ytzrakjgcvCfLCCZp/contra-wentworth-on-physical-attractiveness-for-men#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Claude, Author of the Humanitas](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wRNJZz2iYrfDaSDdz/claude-author-of-the-humanitas)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>In the wee hours of Memorial Day, my friends and I stayed up past 4:30 AM California time to listen to the announcement of Pope Leo’s first encyclical,&nbsp;</span><i><span>Magnifica Humanitas</span></i><span>, on safeguarding the human person in the time of artificial intelligence. We were excited albeit sleepy, eagerly anticipating the event and upcoming essay by the world’s foremost religious authority on a question so central to our world. Still we were an odd audience for this presentation: none of us are practicing Catholics, and most of us didn’t really know what to expect.</span></p><p><span>I thought&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.vatican.va/content/leo-xiv/en/speeches/2026/may/documents/20260525-presentazione-enciclica.html"><span>Pope Leo’s own speech</span></a><span>&nbsp;was good, and addressed the current moment in AI with some of the seriousness it deserves. I thought the other speeches, including by Chris Olah, were less impressive. But that’s okay, I’m not the target audience!</span></p><p><span>A specific cardinal’s point struck me, however:</span></p><p><span>Cardinal Parolin made much of a specific prepositional choice in the subtitle: “</span><i><span>sulla custodia della persona umana nel tempo dell’intelligenza artificiale,</span></i><span>“ which the live translator translated to something like “on the safeguarding of the human person&nbsp;</span><i><span>in the time of</span></i><span>&nbsp;AI,” and not “</span><i><span>sull’intelligenza artificiale</span></i><span>“ – “on AI.”</span></p><p><span>This was supposed to be a big deal. “</span><i><span>In the time of AI”</span></i><span>&nbsp;supposedly centers the human person in the theological narrative, while a mere first papal encyclical&nbsp;</span><i><span>on</span></i><span>&nbsp;AI focuses too much on the technology itself and not on human and societal reactions. A fascinating position!</span></p><p><span>Though as my subsequent analysis will demonstrate, perhaps a more apt preposition here is “</span><i><span>by</span></i><span>.” As in, the world’s first papal encyclical written in large part&nbsp;</span><i><span>by</span></i><span>&nbsp;AI.</span></p><p><span>My article has the following claims, each of which I hope to convince you of:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Significant fractions of the recent papal encyclical are written by AI. I provide multiple lines of evidence for this.</span></li><li value="2"><span>We can corroborate the vibes and tonal indications with statistical evidence. Phrases and punctuation much more commonly used by AI are much more present in this papal encyclical than past encyclicals.</span></li><li value="3"><span>The best commercially available AI detector, Pangram, notes that some paragraphs are between 40% and 100% AI, while most paragraphs appear to be 0% AI.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ol><li value="1"><span>This is unlikely to be a false positive:</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ol><li value="1"><span>0% of paragraphs in past encyclicals I backtested are registered as AI.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Pangram in general has a very low false positive rate</span></li></ol></li></ol></li><li value="4"><span>This is overall very unlikely to be a translation artifact (including AI translation). We again have multiple lines of evidence:</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="5"><ol><li value="1"><span>All the most prominent signs of AI I observed in English are preserved verbatim in the Italian version, as well as in other translations.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The Italian version of the current encyclical also gets flagged as AI by Pangram (actually more so than the English version), though I’m not aware of academic research or rigorous testing of Pangram’s service when applied to Italian)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Backtesting AI translation of past encyclicals get 0% on Pangram</span></li></ol></li><li value="5"><span>The specific AI used is most likely Claude, judging by both textual and circumstantial evidence.</span></li><li value="6"><span>Different sections of the encyclical have very different rates of apparent AI usage. This indicates to me that some cardinals used AI assistance for this encyclical and many (probably including Pope Leo himself) don’t.</span></li><li value="7"><span>Each individual piece of evidence might be explained away, but the&nbsp;</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consilience"><span>consilience</span></a><span>&nbsp;of evidence across multiple angles and sources is in my opinion very hard to dismiss collectively.</span></li></ol><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!9Anp!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F9b47972a-a3a4-489e-9dc7-9267909b2531_700x467.png" target="_blank"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wRNJZz2iYrfDaSDdz/vefkixzavl31erhze4up" /></a></p><h1><span>Significant fractions of the recent papal encyclical are written by AI</span></h1><p><span>I was initially very excited to read Pope Leo’s first encyclical, a long treatise on maintaining humanity in the age of AI. The intersection between AI and societal response is one of my greatest intellectual and personal interests, and it’s both exciting and a relief for the world’s foremost religious authority to share a substantial interest in my personal and career obsessions.</span></p><p><span>Nonetheless – as I kept reading – certain lines jumped out at me as too smooth, too triadic, too…&nbsp;</span><i><span>inhuman</span></i><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>“Technology has the power to heal, connect, educate and protect our common home; but it can also divide, exclude and generate new forms of injustice. In the abstract, technology in and of itself is not a solution to humanity’s problems, just as it is not inherently evil. In practice, however, technology is never neutral, because it takes on the characteristics of those who devise, finance, regulate and use it.”</span></p><p><span>“We must, then, avoid the “Babel syndrome,” namely the idolatry of profit that sacrifices the weak, a uniformity that neutralizes differences, and the pretense that a single language — even a digital one — can translate everything, including the mystery of the person, into data and performance. The risk of dehumanization — of building a future that excludes God and reduces the other to a means — is an ancient and ever-new temptation that today takes on a technical guise.”</span></p><p><span>“A dialogue with such kinds of knowledge does not diminish the power of the Gospel. On the contrary, it makes it possible to identify with greater clarity what genuinely fosters the lives of individuals and communities.”</span></p><p><span>“give stable form to this insight at the ecclesial and international levels, while bearing in mind the growing gap between rich and poor countries and the need for policies that genuinely promote more humane living conditions for all.”</span></p><p><span>“We cannot be satisfied with merely calling for the moralization of machines — the so-called “alignment” of AI with human values — without also having the courage to insist on a further condition: the possibility of openly discussing the ethical frameworks involved and subjecting them to shared standards of social justice”</span></p></blockquote><p><span>I read AI-generated text as part of my job regularly, and believe I have acquired a very good intuition for discerning AI-generated text from those by humans, including in formal writing (both academic and otherwise). Still, any individual phrase that seems AI-generated can be a false positive on my end, the result of an oversensitive nose for AI</span><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-1" target="_self"><span>1</span></a><span>. However, the sheer density of these phrases and overall tone in specific paragraphs seem implausibly all a random artifact.</span></p><p><span>Still, I can definitely be wrong here, and you should not believe my gut intuitions or judgments of vibes on authority (“Trust me bro”).</span></p><p><span>Intuitions, self-proclaimed expert judgments, and loose verbal reasoning can be a good&nbsp;</span><i><span>starting point</span></i><span>&nbsp;for an investigation, but if we want any confidence in our conclusions, we need to investigate further and more systematically.</span></p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!FAHR!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F80cb88b0-cce3-431d-b7da-bc0836834f25_760x507.png" target="_blank"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wRNJZz2iYrfDaSDdz/ag5ipkhmhai4umuuurxp" /></a></p><h1><span>Statistical Evidence and Tells</span></h1><p><span>Three common and well-known tells in AI writing — sometimes genuinely deployed by humans but nowhere their profligate use by AI — are the regularity of em-dashes, the high frequency of specific words like “genuinely”, and the tendency to repeatedly invoke tricolons.</span></p><p><span>Let’s examine each of them in turn:</span></p><h2><span>Em-dashes</span></h2><p><span>The em-dash (“—”) is punctuation that’s by far most strongly associated with AI. It is also used 127 times in&nbsp;</span><i><span>Magnifica Humanitas</span></i><span>, much more than previous encyclicals.</span></p><p><i><span>Magnifica Humanitas:&nbsp;</span></i><span>127 times em-dash, 6 times en-dash (–), the latter all in citations.</span><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-2" target="_self"><span>2</span></a></p><p><i><span>Dilexit Nos (2024)</span></i><span>: 0 times em-dash, 26 en-dashes, including 2 in citations. Comparatively long document.</span><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-3" target="_self"><span>3</span></a></p><p><i><span>Laudate Deum</span></i><span>&nbsp;(2023): 0 times em-dash, 12 times en-dashes. Much shorter. Also not officially an encyclical.</span></p><p><i><span>Fratelli Tutti&nbsp;</span></i><span>(2020): 0 times em-dash, 46 en-dashes, of which maybe 5-10 are in quotes or citations. Note that this is 50% longer than&nbsp;</span><i><span>Magnifica Humanitas.</span></i></p><p><i><span>Laudato Si’&nbsp;</span></i><span>(2016)</span><i><span>:&nbsp;</span></i><span>0 times em-dash, 25 times en-dash, of which maybe 10 are in citations or quotes (the piece overall appears to have many quotes). Similar length to&nbsp;</span><i><span>Magnifica Humanitas</span></i></p><p><i><span>Lumen Fidei</span></i><span>&nbsp;(2013): 26 times em-dash, 0 times en-dash. Some em-dashes in citations.</span></p><p><span>Note that this comparison actually understates the weirdness of the em-dashes in&nbsp;</span><i><span>Magnifica Humanitas. For example, in</span></i><span>&nbsp;</span><i><span>Lumen Fidei</span></i><span>, many of the em-dashes function similarly to speech colons in standard English. A typical use looks like</span></p><blockquote><p><span>What was handed down by the apostles — as the Second Vatican Council states — “comprises everything that serves to make the people of God live their lives in holiness and increase their faith. In this way the Church, in her doctrine, life and worship, perpetuates and transmits to every generation all that she herself is, all that she believes.”</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Using em-dashes as speech colon replacements is moderately common in formal (human) English writing, but essentially absent in LLM-English. I also did not notice em-dashes used this way in&nbsp;</span><i><span>Magnifica Humanitas&nbsp;</span></i><span>(though with 127 instances, it was annoying to check all of them!)</span></p><h2><span>“Genuinely”</span></h2><p><span>“Genuinely” is a phrase&nbsp;</span><a href="https://matthewvollmer.substack.com/p/i-asked-the-machine-to-tell-on-itself"><span>repeatedly used</span></a><span>&nbsp;by Anthropic’s model Claude. It is extremely obvious to anybody who regularly uses it. It’s gotten so bad that&nbsp;</span><a href="https://github.com/asgeirtj/system_prompts_leaks/blob/main/Anthropic/claude.ai-human-readable.md"><span>in leaked system prompts</span></a><span>, Anthropic attempted to explicitly forbid Claude to use that word!</span></p><blockquote><p><span>1.4 Tone &amp; Formatting</span></p><p><span>[...]</span></p><p><span>Claude avoids saying “genuinely”, “honestly”, or “straightforward”.&nbsp;</span><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-4" target="_self"><span>4</span></a></p></blockquote><p><span>As far as I could tell, this injunction does not and did not work.</span></p><p><span>Indeed, Anthropic’s own “</span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/constitution"><span>Claude Constitution</span></a><span>”, which many people believe to be substantially AI-assisted, used the phrase “genuinely” 33 times and genuine overall 50 times (inclusive).</span></p><h4><span>How often is the phrase “genuinely” used in&nbsp;</span><i><span>Magnifica Humanitas?</span></i></h4><p><span>Less so than in Anthropic documents, but substantially more than past papal writings.</span></p><p><span>Specifically “genuinely” was used 9 times and “genuine” overall (inclusive) 22 times in yesterday’s encyclical, compared to 0 and 5 times, respectively, in&nbsp;</span><i><span>Dilexit Nos,&nbsp;</span></i><span>which is of similar length. Across a number of other encyclicals I scanned, the highest occurrences were 3 and 10, respectively.</span></p><p><span>These tells are all statistical. Any individual instance of “genuine(ly)” is plausibly a result of normal human communicative intent that is, well, genuine. But the sheer frequency of these occurrences, vastly out of accord with prior norms and normal human speech, is strongly suggestive of synthetic origin.</span></p><h4><span>Is this due to subject matter?</span></h4><p><span>An obvious rejoinder you might have is that word choices in essays are naturally not independent of subject matter. And it sure seems like an encyclical on AI might meditate more about genuineness more than other encyclicals! For example, an essay on AI deepfakes might be much more concerned about what makes a video “genuinely human” than an essay on climate change.</span></p><p><span>To investigate this hypothesis, I dived specifically into each use of genuinely in this encyclical:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>[Par 23] “A dialogue with such kinds of knowledge does not diminish the power of the Gospel. On the contrary, it makes it possible to identify with greater clarity what&nbsp;</span><b><span>genuinely</span></b><span>&nbsp;fosters the lives of individuals and communities. Following this perspective, Pope Francis [...] recognizes the importance of listening to scientific research and of encouraging a serious and honest debate among experts while welcoming a diversity of opinions.”</span></p></blockquote><p><span>“Genuinely” does not seem critical here, nor specific to questions of AI and authenticity.</span></p><blockquote><p><span>[Par 35] The establishment of the Pontifical Commission&nbsp;</span><i><span>Iustitia et Pax</span></i><span>&nbsp;should also be seen in this light as an attempt to give stable form to this insight at the ecclesial and international levels, while bearing in mind the growing gap between rich and poor countries and the need for policies that&nbsp;</span><b><span>genuinely</span></b><span>&nbsp;promote more humane living conditions for all.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Also not critical here.</span></p><blockquote><p><span>[Par 40] In his social Encyclical Caritas in Veritate, Pope Benedict XVI sought to reassess and expand the concept of development presented in Populorum Progressio, interpreting it in light of globalization. He noted that such development should translate into “real growth, of benefit to everyone and&nbsp;</span><b><span>genuinely</span></b><span>&nbsp;sustainable.” [42]</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Appears to be in a quote, so will give it a pass.</span><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-5" target="_self"><span>5</span></a></p><blockquote><p><span>[Par 57] Along with a greater awareness of the value of every human person and their rights, recognition of minority rights has also grown. Yet, there is still a long way to go to ensure that the rights of a great many, namely women, are equally and&nbsp;</span><b><span>genuinely</span></b><span>&nbsp;guaranteed throughout the world.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Again, does not seem like “genuinely” was endogenously related to the subject matter</span></p><blockquote><p><span>[Par 100] The artificial imitation of positive human communication — words of advice, empathy, friendship and even love — can be engaging and at times&nbsp;</span><b><span>genuinely</span></b><span>&nbsp;helpful. However, for less discerning users, it can also be misleading, creating the illusion of a relationship with a real personal subject. When words are simulated, they do not build genuine relationships, but only their appearance. The artificial imitation of care or support can become particularly risky when it enters contexts where real relationships and emotional bonds are lacking. Here, the danger is not so much that a person may believe they are communicating with another person, but rather that they may gradually lose the very desire to form genuine human connections.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>…you get the idea.</span><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-6" target="_self"><span>6</span></a></p><p><span>Indeed, of all 9 instances of “genuinely” in the encyclical, only the last use (“When people come to believe that nothing is genuinely true and that principles are hollow words, then the fuse in their hearts is lit for new eruptions of intolerance and aggression.”) seem semantically critical. If we drop that and the Pope Benedict quote we’re left with 7/9 suspicious uses.</span></p><p><span>Again, to be clear any individual instance is plausibly normal, authentic, genuine. However the statistical pattern of the repeated invocations is quite suspicious!</span></p><h3><span>Is this just a personality quirk of Pope Leo XIV specifically?</span></h3><p><span>Another possibility you might have is that maybe this is just a personality/stylistic quirk of Pope Leo XIV specifically? Maybe he just genuinely likes the word?</span></p><p><span>Lord knows I too have odd personality quirks in writing, some of which have an&nbsp;</span><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/im-suing-anthropic-personality"><span>unfortunate resemblance to AI</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Ultimately, I think this is plausible but unlikely. First of all, popes&nbsp;</span><a href="https://medium.com/@frdbg70/i-was-a-ghostwriter-for-the-pope-d6950e5ec379"><span>don’t typically draft the text of their own encyclicals</span></a><span>&nbsp;that much. So it’s unlikely that stylistic quirks as specific as adverbial usage will bleed out to the final drafts as much. In contrast, I’m much more open to higher level constructs like the imagery, themes, or favorite Bible passages being much more prominent in some pope’s encyclicals than others.</span></p><p><span>Further, the specific phrases used are often next to other suspicious “AI tells” (more on that later).</span></p><p><span>Unfortunately, I don’t have easy access to many (pre-papacy) writings by Pope Leo to test against this alternative hypothesis. However, I did find Chapter 2 (“The Authority of the Local Prior”) of his 1987 PhD thesis&nbsp;</span><a href="https://digital.library.villanova.edu/files/vudl:1224849/MASTER"><span>here</span></a><span>. In 14 pages (roughly the size of the post you’re reading), the future Pope Leo’s chapter has no uses of “genuine” or “genuinely,” and 0 em-dashes in his own prose.</span><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-7" target="_self"><span>7</span></a></p><p><span>(I welcome extensions of my analysis by people with access to the full&nbsp;</span><a href="https://muse.jhu.edu/book/138641"><span>thesis</span></a><span>&nbsp;in print).</span></p><h2><span>Tricolon density</span></h2><p><span>A common mark of LLM writing is the repeated invocation of tricolons: a series of three parallel words, phrases, or clauses used for rhetorical effect.</span></p><p><span>I noticed quite a few invocations of the tricolons in&nbsp;</span><i><span>Magnifica Humanitas.&nbsp;</span></i><span>It was especially notable in sections that otherwise had other tells of AI.</span><br /><br /><br /><br /><span>Unfortunately, unlike “genuinely” or em-dashes, this is harder to directly observe or baseline, as I can’t use the automatic “find” feature on chrome, it’s annoying to count by hand, and there are numerous edge-cases.</span></p><p><span>Nonetheless, I attempted to use my AI Agent Claude Code ( Claude Opus 4.7 1M XHigh) to give it a good college try, testing&nbsp;</span><i><span>Magnifica Humanitas against&nbsp;</span></i><span>3 encyclicals</span><i><span>&nbsp;</span></i><span>by Francis, 2 by Benedict, 1 jointly by Benedict and Francis and 1 by Leo XIII (who wrote&nbsp;</span><i><span>Rerum Novarum</span></i><span>, which the current encyclical on AI is supposedly strongly based on).</span></p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!RAeJ!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fac481801-252f-4cb0-8c0a-e0c8c2234c42_2048x885.png" target="_blank"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wRNJZz2iYrfDaSDdz/bdox7s4sgcrfhkqisxdo" /></a></p><p><i><span>Caption: Note the easy and natural use of em-dashes above. This is how the AIs naturally speak!</span></i></p><p><span>I think this is&nbsp;</span><i><span>partial</span></i><span>&nbsp;confirmation of my hypothesis. Strict tricolons seem noticeably more prominent in pope Leo XIV’s writings than that of past popes we tested against.</span></p><p><span>Unfortunately (for my hypothesis) there is also substantial variation in the encyclicals authored/commissioned by previous popes. In particular, tricolons are much more common in writings by Benedict than by Francis. So this simple test is suggestive but does not rule out normal human variation.</span></p><p><span>Further, the LLM scan is a rough estimate. There’s inherent subjectivity in a question of triadic markers (unlike more direct vocabulary or punctuation tells). I welcome replication of my attempts here, either via a different AI agent methodology, or (preferably) someone more patient than me willing to manually count and verify this.</span></p><p><span>Do we have other sources of evidence to look at? Yes, we can use automated AI detectors, specifically Pangram.</span></p><h1><span>Pangram analysis</span></h1><p><a href="http://pangram.com/"><span>Pangram</span></a><span>&nbsp;is by far the&nbsp;</span><a href="https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=5407424"><span>best commercially available AI detector</span></a><span>. It is much better than other AI detectors, so much so that other ones are almost useless in comparison. In particular, Pangram optimizes very hard for getting a false positive rate of nearly zero, while being more okay with false negatives.</span></p><p><span>This means that if you see text online that Pangram flags as AI, you should have very high confidence that it’s AI. In contrast, if you have some text that Pangram flags as 100% human, you should still be appropriately skeptical, especially if you’re otherwise suspicious. So how did yesterday’s encyclical do?</span></p><p><span>When I&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.pangram.com/history/eee1ba42-60aa-4d26-b654-ba730cd66715?ucc=YPlSqpgjVXy"><span>pasted the first twenty paragraphs of the encyclical in</span></a><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-8" target="_self"><span>8</span></a><span>, Pangram flags 11% of it as AI.</span></p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!mrUR!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Fc5a138ec-9c15-4ef0-825d-77adaa31d85a_2048x1201.png" target="_blank"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wRNJZz2iYrfDaSDdz/zgrrght3hxtzoerhx1r8" /></a></p><p><span>In particular, Pangram is very suspicious of Paragraphs 7-8.</span></p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!39gX!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Febbe8ee0-6fef-45c1-b1d9-333895527ed0_2048x1139.png" target="_blank"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wRNJZz2iYrfDaSDdz/akcmrfxozj4dndbbybb6" /></a></p><p><span>For what it’s worth, I too was rather suspicious of this section. “It was an impressive feat: a single language, a single technology, a single direction.” sounded just a bit too neat to me.</span></p><p><span>Spot-checking different sections of the encyclical, we see a repeated pattern.</span></p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!TmKp!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F1021646f-6681-492e-8d94-0b012c803776_2048x1269.png" target="_blank"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wRNJZz2iYrfDaSDdz/auaogvg5xntsstb7woio" /></a></p><p><span>Some sections register as essentially 0% AI, while others seem much more AI-y.</span></p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!jmA1!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Ff6a3418c-80b8-4aef-9fa6-a44c014b932a_2048x1301.png" target="_blank"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wRNJZz2iYrfDaSDdz/bbu0jjfx5nos80jh0xuq" /></a></p><p><span>This indicates to me that some cardinals who contributed to the encyclical used AI assistance heavily and most (probably including Pope Leo himself) did not. More on this later.</span></p><h2><span>Comparison to other encyclicals</span></h2><p><span>You might naturally be skeptical of Pangram’s analysis here. After all, maybe you haven’t heard of Pangram (or myself) until today. And besides, Pangram’s likely trained on internet data and its main use case is detecting AI in blog posts and social media posts and movie reviews and academic papers and so forth. What evidence do we have that it’s suited for a task as off-distribution as papal encyclicals? Maybe the detector’s just befuddled by unusually pious tokens?</span></p><p><span>To test this hypothesis, I&nbsp;</span><a href="https://x.com/LinchZhang/status/2059025561218167085"><span>ran Pangram on the previous 4 encyclicals</span></a><span>. The first 20 paragraphs on all of them register as 100% human, all with high confidence:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wRNJZz2iYrfDaSDdz/bvsdhoxnmgzzspszwzqo" /><figcaption><span>Pangram on some past encyclicals</span></figcaption><figcaption><span>Pangram on some past encyclicals</span></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wRNJZz2iYrfDaSDdz/st5jvlybcf0ujglnqvvu" /><figcaption><span>Pangram on some past encyclicals</span></figcaption><figcaption><span>Pangram on some past encyclicals</span></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wRNJZz2iYrfDaSDdz/j3mmhufkvjgdviniicxg" /><figcaption><span>Pangram on some past encyclicals</span></figcaption><figcaption><span>Pangram on some past encyclicals</span></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wRNJZz2iYrfDaSDdz/ifnzot66hmtauwwwfp1e" /><figcaption><span>Pangram on some past encyclicals</span></figcaption><figcaption><span>Pangram on some past encyclicals</span></figcaption></figure><p><span>I also tested writings by Pope Benedict and John Paul in case Pope Francis had an unusually human touch. And against encyclicals by Pope Leos XIII and XII in case Pangram is prejudiced against Leos. All 100% human, as expected.</span></p><h2><span>Comparison to Pope Leo XIV’s speech</span></h2><p><span>I also&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.pangram.com/history/e785f8ba-5a46-45b5-afad-e2f46343e07d?ucc=YPlSqpgjVXy"><span>tested it against a transcript</span></a><span>&nbsp;of Pope Leo’s&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.vatican.va/content/leo-xiv/en/speeches/2026/may/documents/20260525-presentazione-enciclica.html"><span>speech</span></a><span>&nbsp;announcing yesterday’s encyclical. 100% Human on Pangram. This is evidence that Pope Leo himself and/or his primary speechwriter does not use AI to draft his speeches.</span></p><p><span>(Incidentally, “genuine(ly)” appears 0 times in the transcript. Though em-dashes appear three times).</span></p><h2><span>Sidebar: Pangram has a very low false positive rate in general</span></h2><p><span>I think the encyclical evidence specifically should be quite convincing. But separately, I also strongly believe Pangram has a very low false positive rate in general. I observed this both in&nbsp;</span><a href="https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=5407424"><span>academic research</span></a><span>&nbsp;and my own tests:</span></p><p><span>I’ve tested writings that I’m very confident is not AI (e.g. writings by myself, or from before 2021) dozens if not hundreds of times against Pangram, and repeatedly gotten 100% Human. This indicates to me that their advertised very low false positive rate is real.</span></p><p><span>In contrast, the false negative rate is much higher: Sometimes I’d ask an AI to read an outline by me and generate a draft, as a test. Pangram only catches that&nbsp;</span><i><span>sometimes</span></i><span>, though my impression is that they’ve gotten better in the last few months.</span></p><p><span>So you should generally trust that text that Pangram flags as AI is probably AI, while continuing to maintain some healthy suspicion of text that Pangram flags as not AI.</span></p><h1><span>Probably not a translation artifact</span></h1><p><span>One potential mitigating factor is that this is a translation artifact: (human) senior church officials wrote the original encyclical in their native language (maybe Italian) and the&nbsp;</span><i><span>translators</span></i><span>, not the writers, were lazy and used substantially AI assistance in translating to English. I cannot rule this hypothesis out but currently think it’s very unlikely.</span></p><h2><span>The same signs of AI I observe in English are essentially preserved verbatim in the Italian version</span></h2><p><span>If the strong AI signs I’ve observed (“tells”) are a result of lazy translators to English, we should expect them to look different in other languages, especially the source language.</span></p><p><span>To test this, I gave all the tells I noticed in the English encyclical as well as the Italian version of the encyclical to two software agents – Claude Opus 4.7 and ChatGPT 5.5 Pro, arguably the strongest commercially available language models out there – to see whether the tells were preserved in Italian. Both agents believe the tells were preserved verbatim (See Claude screenshot below):</span></p><p><a href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!-wMK!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F85973311-f7fc-4e88-9dae-ff1b7a4bb81c_882x1440.png" target="_blank"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wRNJZz2iYrfDaSDdz/khkdxqnn0sel4pdsd3tp" /></a></p><p><span>That said, I don’t speak Italian myself, and cannot personally verify the results. I welcome replications. Suspicious readers who speak Italian, and especially Italian native speakers who are familiar enough with how AI sounds in Italian, should try to verify the work for themselves!</span></p><h2><span>The Italian version of the current encyclical also gets flagged as AI by Pangram</span></h2><p><span>If the English Pangram results are due to overly technophiliac or lazy translators of Italian to English, we should expect that the original Italian results will get 0% while the English translations gets flagged. We&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.pangram.com/history/48b8311e-90db-4f27-8042-30e86104e6b1?ucc=YPlSqpgjVXy"><span>do not observe this</span></a><span>&nbsp;(first twenty paragraphs again).</span></p><p><a href="https://x.com/0xkartr/status/2058993778925490596"><span>Instead</span></a><span>, the Italian flagged sections appear to be a superset of the sections flagged in English. This would naively indicate that more, not less, of the Italian sections were drafted by AI.</span></p><p><span>(See&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GbWwesBnetyiomxEH/many-portions-of-magnifica-humanitas-appear-to-be-ai-written"><span>further analysis</span></a><span>&nbsp;by Daniel Filan)</span></p><p><span>That said, I don’t know how accurate Italian Pangram is. All the academic research I am aware of on Pangram’s accuracy (and my own experiments) are in English.</span></p><h2><span>Backtesting AI translation of past encyclicals get 0% on Pangram</span></h2><p><span>Another angle on the translation artifact hypothesis: Suppose you 100% organically human-write an article and then use AI to translate it. Does this even get flagged by Pangram?</span></p><p><span>As far as I can tell, the answer is no! (H/T Daniel Filan for this methodology)</span></p><p><span>I took a random excerpt of&nbsp;</span><i><span>Fratelli Tutti</span></i><span>&nbsp;in Italian and asked 3 leading frontier models (Gemini 3.1, ChatGPT 5.5, Claude 4.7) to translate it to English, with web search off. Each translation is different from the official Vatican translation (and from each other).</span></p><p><span>Pangram reads all three texts as 100% human. (</span><a href="https://www.pangram.com/history/d40cfa22-9054-470f-afb9-a092a1b01dc1?ucc=YPlSqpgjVXy"><span>Gemini results</span></a><span>,&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.pangram.com/history/0ae0122d-226e-4e92-ac34-cfb4c0d3694a?ucc=YPlSqpgjVXy"><span>Claude results</span></a><span>,</span><a href="https://www.pangram.com/history/d4de4e12-3d81-40da-b734-a3dc9f572cd1?ucc=YPlSqpgjVXy"><span>&nbsp;ChatGPT results</span></a><span>)</span></p><p><span>This indicates to me further evidence that any Pangram-detected signs of AI in&nbsp;</span><i><span>Magnifica Humanitas&nbsp;</span></i><span>come from stylistic features that are present across languages, rather than artifacts plausibly introduced by AIs in translation.</span></p><p><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/claude-author-of-the-humanitas?utm_source=substack&amp;utm_medium=email&amp;utm_content=share&amp;action=share&amp;token=eyJ1c2VyX2lkIjoyODA1MjQsInBvc3RfaWQiOjE5OTMxNjYxMywiaWF0IjoxNzc5ODExMjY0LCJleHAiOjE3ODI0MDMyNjQsImlzcyI6InB1Yi00NjM3NjAzIiwic3ViIjoicG9zdC1yZWFjdGlvbiJ9.knUE2C83wmUj6n_ZA16LukdWpovEkXxLJ-1GKZJtOQ8"><span>Share</span></a></p><h1><span>The specific AI used is likely Claude</span></h1><p><span>The AI I use the most often for work is Anthropic’s Claude. I have a decent sense of its underlying rhythm, how it argues, and its favorite vocabulary and syntactic choices.</span><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-9" target="_self"><span>9</span></a></p><p><span>I believe I identified the same voice of Claude in the recent papal encyclical about safeguarding the human person in the age of artificial intelligence. Somewhat ironic, considering.</span></p><p><span>Unfortunately, my primary reasons for believing this are somewhat idiosyncratic and inscrutable, and thus more likely to be wrong. And the “Claude authorship” hypothesis is of course overall less important to nail than the “AI authorship” hypothesis, all things considered.</span></p><p><span>Still, I want to offer some textual evidence favoring Claude over other models</span><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-10" target="_self"><span>10</span></a><span>.</span></p><h2><span>Textual</span></h2><p><span>The aforementioned “genuinely” strongly bears the fingerprints of Claude. It is very much the house style of Claude/Anthropic and regularly used in both internal and external Anthropic communications about Claude’s nature and in Claude’s repeated outputs. To be honest, I see “genuinely” so much these days that the word no longer feels like a real word to me and I’ve completely lost the ability to spell the word correctly by myself, never mind appreciating it in context.</span></p><p><span>Maybe there’s a metaphor in there about authenticity in the age of AI? Nah, I’m probably overthinking it.</span></p><p><span>Regardless, the repeated uses of genuinely in&nbsp;</span><i><span>Magnifica Humanitas&nbsp;</span></i><span>is strongly suggestive of Claude-specific fingerprints.</span></p><h3><span>Against ChatGPT</span></h3><p><span>Applying the same analysis against ChatGPT fingerprints gives us a negative result. The words that I most associate with ChatGPT (“</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.11385"><span>delve</span></a><span>”, “meticulous,” “tapestry”, “</span><a href="https://openai.com/index/where-the-goblins-came-from/"><span>goblins</span></a><span>”) over other LLMs all show up zero times in the English encyclical.</span></p><p><span>This is moderate evidence against significant ChatGPT usage.</span></p><p><span>I don’t know enough about the tells that are specific to other models (Gemini, Grok, Mistral, DeepSeek,&nbsp;</span><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/fine-tuning-borges"><span>Kimi</span></a><span>, etc). I welcome replications!</span></p><h1><span>Different sections of the encyclical have very different rates of apparent AI usage</span></h1><p><span>Significant fractions of the text in both English&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GbWwesBnetyiomxEH/many-portions-of-magnifica-humanitas-appear-to-be-ai-written"><span>and Italian</span></a><span>&nbsp;are flagged as AI. This varies significantly from section to section and paragraph to paragraph.</span></p><p><span>Some paragraphs in Pangram, and also by visual inspection, are clearly AI, while others seem clearly not AI.</span></p><p><span>My understanding is that popes&nbsp;</span><a href="https://medium.com/@frdbg70/i-was-a-ghostwriter-for-the-pope-d6950e5ec379"><span>don’t usually draft</span></a><span>&nbsp;the majority of the text of their encyclicals themselves.</span></p><p><span>Between these two facts, this indicates to me that some cardinals heavily used AI assistance for this encyclical and many (probably including Pope Leo himself) didn’t.</span></p><p><span>My tentative hypothesis is that&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.ncronline.org/vatican/pope-leo-tells-priests-not-use-ai-write-homilies-or-seek-likes-tiktok"><span>Pope Leo does not approve</span></a><span>&nbsp;of the AI usage in encyclicals, and plausibly was not even aware of significant AI usage in his own encyclical! Quite unfortunate if true.</span></p><h1><span>Conclusion</span></h1><p><span>I attempted to provide a number of different angles and evidence for the conclusion that the papal encyclical on safeguarding the human person&nbsp;</span><i><span>in the age of AI</span></i><span>&nbsp;is actually an article in large part neither&nbsp;</span><i><span>on</span></i><span>&nbsp;nor&nbsp;</span><i><span>in</span></i><span>&nbsp;AI, but&nbsp;</span><i><span>by</span></i><span>&nbsp;AI. Any individual method might be flawed, but I believe the consilience of evidence is very strongly suggestive, perhaps even overwhelming.</span></p><p><span>Nonetheless, I welcome good-faith debate and corrections. Please feel free to comment with contrary evidence and replications.</span></p><p><span>We’re soon entering&nbsp;</span><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/simplest-case-ai-catastrophe"><span>a time of unprecedented danger in the world</span></a><span>. Like children with flamethrowers, or Bronze Age peasants attempting to build the Tower of Babel, humanity is messing with forces we cannot hope to understand or control.</span></p><p><span>In this new age of AI, getting provenance genuinuely right isn’t just a question of human authenticity — it’s a matter of life and death.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-anchor-1" target="_self"><span>1</span></a></p><p><span>Though in previous attempts to validate my intuition systematically, I have a substantially higher false negative rate than false positive rate, suggesting I’m not sensitive&nbsp;</span><i><span>enough</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-anchor-2" target="_self"><span>2</span></a></p><p><span>Technically 6 times, but all 6 in citations (eg “</span><i><span>cf</span></i><span>&nbsp;</span><i><span>Neh 2–6</span></i><span>, [123] Cf. Dicastery for the Doctrine of the Faith – Dicastery for Culture and Education”)</span></p><p><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-anchor-3" target="_self"><span>3</span></a></p><p><span>Unedited AI texts use em-dashes much more often than en-dashes. The former is much more directly a sign of AI than the latter. However, it’s common for people hiding AI writing to change em-dashes to en-dashes. Additionally, the specific differences between em-dashes and en-dashes could just be random variation of specific stylistic preferences/trends between people. Thus, we should probably count em-dashes and en-dashes together in the analysis, though em-dashes over en-dashes is still some (nonzero) evidence.</span></p><p><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-anchor-4" target="_self"><span>4</span></a></p><p><span>I asked Claude to review this section. It says the injunction to avoid “genuinely” is no longer in its system prompt as of Opus 4.7 (and has no direct evidence of past prompts), though it believes the previous/github report is credible enough for Opus 4.6.</span></p><p><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-anchor-5" target="_self"><span>5</span></a></p><p><span>This is not strictly zero information. Given Claude’s demonstrated predilection for genuinely, we can imagine this specific passage was chosen by Claude to quote.</span></p><p><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-anchor-6" target="_self"><span>6</span></a></p><p><span>Interestingly, here the instances of “genuine” (“genuine relationships”, “genuine human connections”) seemed to carry actual semantic content, while “genuinely helpful” did not. So despite its placement in the rest of the paragraph, I don’t think “genuinely” is semantically relevant here.</span></p><p><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-anchor-7" target="_self"><span>7</span></a></p><p><span>If you’re very literal about it, there are 2 em-dashes in the chapter. One of them is in a large quote-block by someone else. The other is in the footnote “Robert F. Prevost, O.S.A. — Canon lawyer working in Chulucanas, Peru.” Neither invocations are semantic in the main text, and the latter likely added by an editor.</span></p><p><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-anchor-8" target="_self"><span>8</span></a></p><p><span>The whole encyclical is too long for Pangram.</span></p><p><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-anchor-9" target="_self"><span>9</span></a></p><p><span>I did not use AI to draft or write any of the text in this post, including for sentences deliberately engineered to sound like AI.</span></p><p><a href="https://substack.com/home/post/p-199316613#footnote-anchor-10" target="_self"><span>10</span></a></p><p><span>Note that I’m only moderately confident in the presence, and to a lesser degree, the substantial contribution, of Claude’s fingerprints on the recent encyclical. I can’t rule out that other AIs were&nbsp;</span><i><span>also</span></i><span>&nbsp;</span><b><span>separately</span></b><span>&nbsp;involved. I’m also only moderately confident in my conclusion that it’s Claude: all the AIs are kinda similar to each other, so differentiating between them is much harder than detecting the presence of AI at all.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wRNJZz2iYrfDaSDdz/claude-author-of-the-humanitas#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [RTMH: Pope Leo’s Magnifica Humanitas on AI](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RZeg98Tdrq47vbjva/rtmh-pope-leo-s-magnifica-humanitas-on-ai)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>His holiness has spoken, frequently about AI. At eighty two pages of length.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.ewtnnews.com/vatican/full-text-of-magnifica-humanitas-read-pope-leo-xiv-s-first-encyclical">The full Magnifica Humanitas can be found here.</a></p>
<p>I am very happy that Pope Leo takes these issues seriously, and is sharing his views, and bringing a form of moral clarity, even with all the flaws and central errors. <a href="https://x.com/lisperati/status/2059064648482382308">More people with voice should share their views</a> in this way, even when I disagree.</p>
<p>It’s a weird document. Much of it is not about AI at all.</p>
<p>I do agree with the Pope’s most basic point on AI, which is that AI can be what we make of it. That we can steer this technology, determine how it is developed and used, and this can determine whether we get a good or not so good future. We cannot purely leave this to market incentives and strategic pressures. Yes, very much so.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25305"></span>


</div>
<p>The central problem is that so much of Leo’s worldview is some combination, to me, of highly alien and highly wrong. You might think that would primarily have a lot to do with him being the Pope and rather Catholic, and being a man of faith, whereas I am not these things.</p>
<p>If so, you would be wrong. That seems to have remarkably little to do with all of this.</p>
<p>There was also a lot of good here, but I was centrally disappointed on three fronts:</p>
<ol>
<li>The central claim, wherein Leo denies that AIs can think or importantly be minds, is wrong, as Olah points out in his statements.</li>
<li>Without the understanding of what AI is capable of becoming, the document effectively only deals with relatively mundane AI dangers and changes, although that on its own is still rather quite a lot to deal with and discuss.</li>
<li>Pope Leo subscribes to a view of economics and a System of the World that I believe are simply wrong about what actions and systems cause what consequences, subscribing to what is effectively an institutionalist, European technocrat, left-wing social justice socialist labor-centered perspective, especially with treating the role of the economy as creating and protecting ‘good jobs.’ To his credit and that of previous Popes, they do understand the central value of development and growth, but they reject the ways we get there in practice.</li>
</ol>
<p>You want some amount of people pushing in the direction of peace and mercy and dialogue and watching out for the poor and disempowered, and calling on us to do more for our fellow man. So as part of a balanced bigger picture, this could be actively good, but Europe has shown us the peril of lacking this balance.</p>
<p>This post will summarize the whole thing, going number by number, with occasional commentary focused on the key AI section in the middle.</p>
<p>Anthropic cofounder Chris Olah visited the Vatican for the occasion. He endorsed the document, but also offered remarks disagreeing with the central point (paragraph 99). I’ll discuss that afterwards, along with how the media viewed the release.</p>


<h4>Table of Contents</h4>


<ol>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/199313939/a-brief-history-of-magnifica-humanitas">A Brief History of Magnifica Humanitas.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/199313939/economic-models-very-different-from-our-own">Economic Models Very Different From Our Own.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/199313939/a-new-jerusalem">A New Jerusalem.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/199313939/so-sayeth-the-pope-on-ai">So Sayeth The Pope (on AI).</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/199313939/the-case-against-human-achievement">The Case Against Human Achievement.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/199313939/truth-justice-and-the-vatican-way">Truth, Justice and the Vatican Way.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/199313939/they-took-our-jobs">They Took Our Jobs.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/199313939/what-is-not-fair-in-love-and-war">What Is Not Fair In Love and War.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/199313939/come-ye-christian-faithful">Come Ye Christian Faithful.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/199313939/the-other-missing-mood">The Other Missing Mood.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/199313939/pope-given-about-five-words">Pope Given About Five Words.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/199313939/0the-anthropic-principle">0The Anthropic Principle.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/199313939/claude-can-read-your-code">Claude Can Read Your Code.</a></li>
</ol>


<h4>A Brief History of Magnifica Humanitas</h4>


<p>Chapters 1 and 2 lay out some history. Any Pope is going to be a huge history nerd and set all this in its historical context. Leo does not disappoint.</p>
<ol>
<li>Christianity is The Way.</li>
<li>Christianity is The Way.</li>
<li>If you see something [on Earth that matters], say something.</li>
<li>Tech can be good or bad. We decide which.</li>
<li>We must regulate, and also other things. Tech power grows private.</li>
<li>Which way, modern man?</li>
<li>Tower of Babel means various good things are actually bad. Confused.</li>
<li>Rebuilding of Jerusalem via Nehemiah shows value of diversity? Confused.</li>
<li>Tech can be good or bad. We decide which.</li>
<li>We must avoid ‘Babel syndrome’ and instead choose Way of Nehemiah. Diversity. Avoid a common language. Good things like peace, justice, fraternity, God.</li>
<li>God is The Way.</li>
<li>Do not try to fix the limits and weaknesses of humanity. That can lead to inequality. True fulfillment is about the least well off people.</li>
<li>From each according to his ability, to each according to his need.</li>
<li>Speak no evil. All power to our socialist central planners.</li>
<li>Stay human, my friends. Listen.</li>
<li>God is The Way. Work only for the common good. Get your ‘hands dirty.’</li>
<li>We must go over the history of documents like this.</li>
<li>We must first review fundamental principles, and why life on Earth matters.</li>
<li>The Church should try to help improve the world. Tikkun olam.</li>
<li>The Church no longer gets to tell the State what to do.</li>
<li>Second Vatican Council affirmed that, but we’ll still try and give you a push.</li>
<li>The Church still speaks with moral authority.</li>
<li>The Church loves science, truth, goodness, beauty, if you do too we’re friends.</li>
<li>The Church has moral authority but science has authority over other things.</li>
<li>Speak and be open to truth. Welcome diversity. Avoid power and violence.</li>
<li>The Church does not have all the answers in the Earthly realm.</li>
<li>The Church social doctrine is an ongoing process.</li>
<li>We now review the history of the Church social doctrine.</li>
<li>Leo XIII was first to have the Church address these questions.</li>
<li>Leo XIII chose dignity and workers over profits but defends private property. We continue to affirm the primacy of labor and justice. We cannot stand aside.</li>
<li>Pius XI in 1931 reaffirmed all this, warned against both unlimited collectivism and unlimited competition and concentration of power, and established the principle of subsidiarity: Everything should be handled as locally as possible.</li>
<li>Pius XII appealed to natural law, affirmed labor, opposed force and inequality.</li>
<li>John XXIII affirmed all this, truth, love, freedom, justice, et al.</li>
<li>Second Vatican Council engaged with the world, affirmed religious freedom.</li>
<li>Paul VI equated peace with universal prosperity, justice, equality.</li>
<li>Paul VI said Gospel called for everyone to enjoy the fruits of development.</li>
<li>John Paul II affirmed central importance of work and ‘fair wages.’</li>
<li>John Paul II hated underdevelopment and favoring of national interests.</li>
<li>John Paul II affirmed democracy, subordinated markets to moral law.</li>
<li>Benedict XVI demanded economic activity serve common good, be ‘real growth.’</li>
<li>Benedict XVI centered charity, evaluation of development as common good.</li>
<li>Francis affirms Church is social, human lives matter, the poor matter.</li>
<li>Francis talked about the environment, linked it to the poor, waste, justice.</li>
<li>Francis proposed we all work for common good, said Jesus is The Way.</li>
<li>Church Social Doctrine responds to what happens in the world.</li>
</ol>
<p>On to chapter 2, foundations and principles of church doctrine.</p>
<ol>
<li>We reflect on: Common good, subsidiarity, solidarity, social justice.</li>
<li>You should implement these principles in your daily life.</li>
<li>God is love. The trinity is love. And relationship and sharing.</li>
<li>Jesus cared about people and wanted us to work to make the world better.</li>
<li>Humans are in God’s image, have dignity, relate to God, develop.</li>
<li>Humans all have dignity, need not justify or earn their worth. All are equal. We cannot value more those who produce or do more. Rights are inalienable.</li>
<li>Dignity can be social, moral, existential, ontological. All equal and inalienable.</li>
<li>This dignity, of every human, is infinite.</li>
<li>Human rights are an expression of human dignity.</li>
<li>Human rights are thus inviolable. Abortion and euthanasia are gravely wrong.</li>
<li>Rights often aren’t honored and are at risk, often due to tech and power.</li>
<li>Women’s rights must be honored, including equal access to all the things.</li>
<li>Individuals and families, and them meeting their needs, are what matters.</li>
<li>The common good and human dignity must shape our lives.</li>
<li>Common good means letting all people ‘reach fulfillment.’</li>
<li>Self-interest cannot make a better world for families.</li>
<li>Life to a people comes from pursuit of the common good via shared vision.</li>
<li>The State must organize society in pursuit of common good, think long term.</li>
<li>Nations must cooperate towards this common good. Divides widen.</li>
<li>Using Earth’s resources to benefit the few is an affront to God.</li>
<li>You have the right to private property, but only in service of the common good.</li>
<li>Patents, algorithms, digital platforms, technology infrastructure and data also now count as ‘Earth’s resources’ and must be routed in service of common good.</li>
<li>Affirmation of subsidiarity.</li>
<li>Family and individual must not be subsumed by the state.</li>
<li>Affirmation of subsidiarity. Endorsement of voluntary organizations.</li>
<li>Technology companies violate subsidiarity, must serve the common good.</li>
<li>States collectively must ensure local voice and choice, avoid private tech control.</li>
<li>Subsidiarity requires solidarity. We are all in this together.</li>
<li>Full solidarity must be a conscious choice.</li>
<li>Solidarity is a principle and a virtue, requires modest shared ways of life, sacrifice.</li>
<li>Reiterated claims to collective decision making over technology.</li>
<li>Jesus was a big social justice guy, which means everyone gets dignity.</li>
<li>Start with the poorest among us.</li>
<li>Systems create inequality and are unjust. Boo wars, colonialism, discrimination, violence against entire peoples and ‘exploitation.’</li>
<li>Social justice also involves all aspects of digital technologies. People not profits.</li>
<li>Social justice litmus test: Migrants, refugees, those forced to move.</li>
<li>Development that does not ‘foster each man and of the whole man’ is ‘inauthentic.’</li>
<li>Development is a duty and a right, including beyond economics.</li>
<li>Development is measured by justice.</li>
<li>Tech is good if and only if it helps people become more humane and fraternal while respecting our common home and future generations.</li>
<li>This doctrine is an extension of the Church.</li>
<li>Subsidiarity is the guiding principle of governance and pastoral life.</li>
<li>Solidarity, for Christians, comes from Christ and the Eucharist.</li>
<li>The Church must face and address its legacy of abuse of power.</li>
</ol>


<h4>Economic Models Very Different From Our Own</h4>


<p>This view, laid out in the first two chapters, is a very different perspective and worldview than my own, and this has remarkably little to do with belief the the divine or a lack thereof.</p>
<p>This is the Socialist perspective on economics and development and opportunity and the importance of equality and disdain for profit and self-interest, with an extreme focus on labor and jobs, which I think is wrong and leads to worse results for everyone.</p>
<p>This is much better than failing to care about humanity’s experience on Earth, or focusing purely on direct aid to the poor, or attempting to outright seize the resources although this doctrine is clearly flirting with quite a lot of this, and the dedication to the value of development is admirable.</p>
<p>The Pope is simply incorrect about where wealth and development come from, and what causes prosperity. He is also wrong about what he sees as a ‘widening gap’ between nations, whereas global inequality has been steadily falling for a while.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2058970412114211107">Dean Ball is exactly correct that Leo is casting himself</a> in the role of a European technocrat throughout. I had exactly the same thought.</p>
<p>Leo both is <a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2058922191278755962">using so many of the talking points of the European technocrats</a>, and also has deeply absorbed their worldview, except with a more left-wing economic bent. This is true no matter how much those points originated with the Church.</p>
